"source_original", "content": "=== PDF page 1 ===\narXiv:2312.12456v1  [cs.LG]  16 Dec 2023\nPowerInfer: Fast Large Language Model Serving with a Consumer-grade GPU\nYixin Song, Zeyu Mi∗, Haotong Xie and Haibo Chen\nInstitute of Parallel and Distributed Systems (IPADS), Shanghai Jiao Tong University\nAbstract\nThis paper introduces PowerInfer,a high-speed Large Lan-\nguage Model (LLM) inference engine on a personal com-\nputer (PC) equipped with a single consumer-grade GPU. The\nkey underlying the design of PowerInfer is exploiting the\nhigh locality inherent in LLM inference, characterized by a\npower-law distribution in neuron activation. This distribution\nindicates that a small subset of neurons, termed hot neurons,\nare consistently activated across inputs, while the majority,\ncold neurons, vary based on speciﬁc inputs. PowerInfer ex-\nploits such an insight to design a GPU-CPU hybrid inference\nengine: hot-activated neurons are preloaded onto the GPU\nfor fast access, while cold-activated neurons are computed on\nthe CPU, thus signiﬁcantly reducing GPU memory demands\nand CPU-GPU data transfers. PowerInfer further integrates\nadaptive predictors and neuron-aware sparse operators, opti-\nmizing the efﬁciency of neuron activation and computational\nsparsity. Evaluation shows that PowerInfer attains an average\ntoken generation rate of 13.20 tokens/s, with a peak of 29.08\ntokens/s, across various LLMs (including OPT-175B) on a\nsingle NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU, only 18% lower than that\nachieved by a top-tier server-grade A100 GPU. This signiﬁ-\ncantly outperforms llama.cpp by up to 11.69× while retain-\ning model accuracy.\n1\nIntroduction\nGenerative large language models (LLMs) have garnered\nattention for their remarkable capabilities in creative writ-\ning, advanced code generation, and sophisticated natural lan-\nguage processing tasks [5,42,49]. These models, widely de-\nployed in data centers equipped with high-end and expensive\nserver-grade GPUs, have signiﬁcantly inﬂuenced our daily\nlives and work practices. Meanwhile, there is an emerging\ntrend of running LLMs on more accessible local platforms,\nparticularly personal computers (PCs) with consumer-grade\nGPUs. This evolution is driven by the need for enhanced\ndata privacy [25], model customization [22], and reduced in-\nference costs [42]. In contrast to data-center deployments,\nwhich prioritize high throughput [18, 37, 47], local deploy-\nments focus on low latency in processing small batches.\nNonetheless, deploying LLMs on consumer-grade GPUs\npresents signiﬁcant challenges due to their substantial mem-\n∗Zeyu Mi (yzmizeyu@sjtu.edu.cn) is the corresponding author.\nory requirements. LLMs, typically functioning as autoregres-\nsive Transformers,sequentially generate text token-by-token,\neach needing to access the entire model consisting of hun-\ndreds of billions of parameters. Therefore, the inference pro-\ncess is fundamentally constrained by the GPU’s memory ca-\npacity. This limitation is particularly acute in local deploy-\nments where the processing of individual requests (often just\none at a time) [6] leaves minimal opportunity for parallel pro-\ncessing.\nExisting approaches to such memory issues include model\ncompression and ofﬂoading. Compression techniques like\nquantization [12, 46], distillation [48], and pruning [23] re-\nduce the model size. However, even deeply compressed\nmodels remain too large for consumer-grade GPUs. For in-\nstance, an OPT-66B model with 4-bit precision demands\napproximately 40GB of memory just to load its parame-\nters [20], exceeding the capacity of even high-end GPUs like\nthe NVIDIA RTX 4090. Model ofﬂoading, which partitions\nthe model between GPU and CPU at the Transformer layer\nlevel [3,14,37]. State-of-the-art systems like llama.cpp [14]\ndistribute layers between CPU and GPU memories, leverag-\ning both for inference, thus reducing the GPU resources re-\nquired. However, this method is hindered by the slow PCIe\ninterconnect and the CPUs’ limited computational capabili-\nties, resulting in high inference latency.\nIn this paper, we argue that the key reason for memory\nissues in LLM inference is the locality mismatch between\nhardware architecture and the characteristics of LLM in-\nference. Current hardware architectures are designed with\na memory hierarchy optimized for data locality. Ideally, a\nsmall, frequently accessed working set should be stored in\nthe GPU, which offers higher memory bandwidth but limited\ncapacity. In contrast, larger, less frequently accessed data are\nbetter suited for CPUs, which provide more extensive mem-\nory capacity but lower bandwidth. Nevertheless, the vast vol-\nume of parameters required for each LLM inference iteration\nleads to a working set that is too large for a single GPU, thus\nimpeding efﬁcient locality exploitation.\nWe have observed that LLM inference inherently exhibits\nhigh locality. Speciﬁcally, during each inference iteration, a\nlimited number of neurons1 are activated, signiﬁcantly inﬂu-\n1This paper deﬁnes a neuron as a speciﬁc row/column in a weight matrix.\n1\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\nencing the outcome of token inference. These activations,\nwhich are input-speciﬁc, can be accurately predicted dur-\ning runtime. For example, in the OPT model, less than 10%\nof the elements in the activation map are non-zero, and\nthese can be predicted with more than 93% accuracy at run-\ntime [21]. Notably, neuron activation in an LLM follows a\nskewed power-law distribution: a small subset of neurons\nconsistently contributes to the majority of activations (over\n80%) across various inputs (hot-activated), while the major-\nity are involved in the remaining activations, which are deter-\nmined based on the inputs at runtime (cold-activated).\nBuilding on the locality insights, we introduce PowerIn-\nfer, an efﬁcient LLM inference system optimized for local\ndeployments using a single consumer-grade GPU. The key\nidea of PowerInfer is to exploit the locality in LLM infer-\nence by assigning the minor hot neurons to the GPU, while\ncold neurons, which constitute the majority, are managed by\nthe CPU. PowerInfer preselects and preloads hot-activated\nneurons onto the GPU ofﬂine and leverages online predic-\ntors during runtime to identify activated neurons. This ap-\nproach allows the GPU and CPU to independently process\ntheir respective sets of neurons, thereby minimizing the need\nfor costly PCIe data transfers.\nHowever, there are signiﬁcant challenges that complicate\nthe design of PowerInfer. First, the online predictors, which\nare essential for identifying active neurons in LLM layers\nand are typically situated on the GPU, occupy a considerable\namount of GPU memory. This memory could otherwise be\nused for the LLM. To address this, PowerInfer introduces an\nadaptive method for constructing smaller predictors for lay-\ners with higher activation sparsity and skewness. This itera-\ntive process reduces the size of the predictors while maintain-\ning their accuracy, thus freeing up GPU memory for LLM\ninferences.\nSecond, leveraging LLM sparsity requires the use of\nsparse operators. Conventional libraries like cuSPARSE [30]\nare not optimal due to their general-purpose design, which in-\ncludes tracking each non-zero element and converting dense\nmatrices into sparse formats [45,51]. In contrast, PowerInfer\ndesigns neuron-aware sparse operators that directly interact\nwith individual neurons, thereby bypassing operations on en-\ntire matrices. This approach enables efﬁcient matrix-vector\nmultiplication at the neuron level and removes the need for\nspeciﬁc sparse format conversions.\nLastly, the optimal placement of activated neurons be-\ntween the GPU and CPU in PowerInfer is a complex task. It\ninvolves evaluating each neuron’s activation rate, intra-layer\ncommunication, and available hardware resources like GPU\nmemory sizes. To effectively manage this, PowerInfer uti-\nlizes an ofﬂine phase to generate a neuron placement pol-\nicy. This policy uses a metric that measures each neuron’s\nimpact on LLM inference outcomes and is framed as an in-\nteger linear programming problem. The policy formulation\nconsiders factors such as neuron activation frequencies and\nthe bandwidth hierarchy of CPU and GPU architectures.\nThe online inference engine of PowerInfer was imple-\nmented by extending llama.cpp with an additional 4,200\nlines of C++ and CUDA code. Its ofﬂine component, com-\nprising a proﬁler and a solver, builds upon the transformers\nframework [44] with approximately 400 lines of Python code.\nPowerInferis compatible with various popular LLM families,\nincluding OPT (7B-175B), LLaMA (7B-70B), and Falcon-\n40B, and supports consumer-grade GPUs like the NVIDIA\nRTX 4090 and NVIDIA RTX 2080Ti.\nPerformance evaluation reveals that PowerInfer, when de-\nployed on a PC equipped with a single NVIDIA RTX\n4090 GPU, delivers an average generation speed of 13.20\ntokens/s for quantized models and 8.32 tokens/s for non-\nquantized models, maintaining model accuracy. These re-\nsults signiﬁcantly surpass llama.cpp’s performance, exhibit-\ning up to 8.00× and 11.69× improvements for quantized and\nnon-quantized models, respectively. Signiﬁcantly, the infer-\nence speed achieved on an NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU (priced\nat approximately $2,000) is only 18% slower compared to\nthe performance on a top-tier A100 GPU (costing around\n$20,000) that can fully accommodate the model. PowerIn-\nfer’s source code is publicly available at https://github.\ncom/SJTU-IPADS/PowerInfer.\n2\nBackground and Motivation\nEmbedding\nDecoding\nTransformer\nTransformer\nEmbedding\nDecoding\nTransformer\nTransformer\nI love reading\nOSDI\nOSDI\nPapers\n…\n…\nEmbedding\nDecoding\nTransformer\nTransformer\npapers\n<EOS>\n…\nPrompt Phase\nGeneration Phase\nFigure 1: The inference procedure of an LLM.\n2.1\nLLM Inference & Architecture\nLLM inference, an autoregressive model, generates each to-\nken based on previous ones. The process, illustrated in Fig-\nure 1, starts with a prompt (e.g., \"I love reading\") and un-\nfolds in two phases: ﬁrst, the prompt phase outputs an initial\ntoken (\"OSDI\"), then the generation phase sequentially pro-\nduces tokens until a maximum limit or an end-of-sequence\n(<EOS>) token is reached. Each token generation, an infer-\nence iteration, requires running the full LLM model.\nThe LLM architecture includes multiple Transformer lay-\ners, each comprising a self-attention and an MLP (Multi-\nLayer Perceptron) block (see Figure 2, left). The self-\nattention block generates embedding vectors by capturing\nthe relationships among input tokens. In this process, dif-\nferent heads focus on extracting distinct feature information.\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\nFC1 Weights\nSelf-Attention Block\nFC1 Layer\nReLU\nFC2 Layer\n…\n×\n=\nReLU\nFC2 Weights\n=\n×\n…\nMLP Block\nFC1 Input\nFC1 Output\nFC2 input\nFC2 Output\nFigure 2: The architecture of a Transformer layer and how neurons\nare sparsely activated in FC1 and FC2 layers due to the ReLU func-\ntion. The neurons that are activated are represented as green rows or\ncolumns encircled by red lines. The output vector from FC1 is then\nsupplied to FC2 as its input vector.\nThe computation results from these different heads are ag-\ngregated and then utilized as the input for the MLP block.\nThe MLP block applies non-linear transformations via fully\nconnected layers and activation functions to reﬁne the input\nsequence representation. The output either advances to sub-\nsequent layers or forms the LLM’s ﬁnal output.\nIn Figure 2 (right), the MLP block’s layers, FC1 and FC2,\ngenerate vectors through matrix multiplication. Each output\nelement comes from the dot product of an input vector and a\nneuron (a row/column in a weight matrix). Activation func-\ntions like ReLU [1] act as gates to selectively retain or dis-\ncard values in a vector, inﬂuencing neuron activations in FC1\nand FC2. For example, ReLU in this ﬁgure ﬁlters out nega-\ntive values, allowing only positively valued neurons in FC1\nto inﬂuence the output. These neurons, which contribute to\nthe output, are considered activated in this paper. Similarly,\nthese values also affect which neurons in FC2 are activated\nand involved in the computation of its output vector.\nActivation Sparsity. Recent studies have revealed that\nLLM inference shows a notable sparsity in neuron activa-\ntion [19,21,50]. For example, we observe that approximately\n80% of neurons in the OPT-30B model remain inactivated\nduring the inference. This phenomenon of activation spar-\nsity exists in both self-attention and MLP blocks. In self-\nattention blocks, nearly half of the attention heads (neurons)\nmake minimal contributions, leading to their high sparsity.\nThe sparsity observed within the MLP blocks is primarily\nattributed to the characteristics of the activation functions.\nCrucially, the activation sparsity is input-speciﬁc,meaning\nthat the activation of speciﬁc neurons is directly inﬂuenced\nby the current input and cannot be predetermined before the\nmodel’s inference iteration begins. While it is not feasible to\nknow which neurons will be activated before the entire model\nruns, it is possible to predict neuron activations a few layers\nin advance within the ongoing model iteration. DejaVu [21],\nfor instance, utilizes MLP-based predictors during inference,\nachieving a remarkable accuracy rate of at least 93% in pre-\ndicting neuron activation.\nL4\nL5\nL6\n…\nLn\nCPU Mem\nGPU Mem\nCPU\nGPU\nL3\nL2\nL1\nPCIe\nL4\n1\n2\nCPU \nResults\nL1\nL2\nL3\n…\nLn-3\nCPU Mem\nGPU Mem\nCPU\nGPU\nLn-2\nLn-1\nLn\nPCIe\nCPU \nResults\n2\n3\n(a) GPU-Centric Oﬄoading\n(b) GPU-CPU Hybrid Oﬄoading\n1\n3\nFigure 3: Typical existing ofﬂoading solutions. (a) shows a GPU-\ncentric approach, while (b) is the CPU-GPU hybrid ofﬂoading ap-\nproach.\n2.2\nOfﬂoading-based LLM Serving\n 0\n 800\n 1600\n 2400\n 3200\n 4000\n1\n4\n8\n16\nLatency per iteration (ms)\nFlexGen\nDejaVu(UM)\nllama.cpp\n(a)\n90\n100\n~\n~\nFlexGen-PCIe\nFlexGen-GPU\nDejaVu(UM)-PCIe\nDejaVu(UM)-GPU\nllama.cpp-CPU\nllama.cpp-GPU\n1\n4\n8\n16\n0\n4\n8\nBreakdown (%)\n0.5\n0.5\n0.5\n0.5\n0.4\n0.4\n0.4\n0.4\n2.4\n1.7\n1.1\n0.6\n~\n~\n(b)\nFigure 4: Performance comparison and analysis for serving OPT-\n30B on NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU. The yellow blocks refer to Flex-\nGen, the gray blocks refer to DejaVu (UM) and the blue blocks refer\nto llama.cpp. (a) The Y-axis indicates execution time for one itera-\ntion and the X-axis represents batch sizes for input. (b) The Y-axis\nindicates the proportion of execution time, and the X-axis indicates\nbatch sizes for input.\nCurrent model compression techniques are inadequate\nfor ﬁtting large language models (LLMs) within resource-\nlimited consumer-grade GPUs. In contrast, the ofﬂoading\ntechnique, which leverages the CPU’s additional computa-\ntional and memory resources, presents a more viable solu-\ntion for accommodating LLMs on such hardware. Figure 3\nillustrates two main ofﬂoading approaches:\nGPU-centric ofﬂoading utilizes CPU memory to store\nportions of the model parameters that exceed the GPU’s ca-\npacity. During each iteration, as depicted in Figure 3a), it pro-\ncesses the parameters located in the GPU memory, transfer-\nring more from the CPU as needed. This strategy enables\nthe inference of LLMs of varying sizes, provided that sufﬁ-\ncient combined CPU memory and hard disk storage are avail-\nable. FlexGen [37] is a typical example that adopts a zig-zag\nscheduling approach to prioritize throughput over latency,\nprocessing batches sequentially for each layer. Nonetheless,\nthis method leads to substantial per-token latency in latency-\nsensitive scenarios (Figure 4a), mainly due to frequent data\ntransfers between GPU and CPU, especially with batch sizes\nof one. Over 99.5% of processing time is consumed by trans-\nferring LLM weights from CPU to GPU, signiﬁcantly im-\npacting overall latency, as illustrated in Figure 4b.\nDejaVu [21] accelerates LLM inference by using activa-\ntion sparsity. It selectively processes only those neurons that\nare predicted to be activated (called predicted neurons for\nbrevity), while bypassing the inactivated ones. However, this\napproach, initially designed for data center inference, strug-\ngles on consumer-grade GPUs that cannot accommodate full-\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\nscale LLMs. The key challenge with DejaVu in such contexts\nstems from the need to frequently transfer activated neurons\nfrom the CPU to the GPU during runtime. For LLMs like\nOPT-30B that exceed GPU memory limits, DejaVu2, albeit\nreducing the computational load on the GPU, is constrained\nby the data transfer procedure (Figure 4a). Consequently, as\nshown in Figure 4a, DejaVu experiences signiﬁcant inference\nlatency, comparable to that of FlexGen.\nHybrid ofﬂoading distributes model parameters between\nGPU and CPU, splitting them at the Transformer layer level\nas shown in llama.cpp [14] (Figure 3b). The CPU processes\nits layers ﬁrst, then sends intermediate results to the GPU\nfor token generation. This ofﬂoading method reduces infer-\nence latency to around 600ms (Figure 4a) by minimizing\ndata transfer and mitigating slow PCIe bandwidth.\nHowever, hybrid ofﬂoading still faces the locality mis-\nmatch issue, leading to suboptimal latency. Each inference\niteration accesses the entire model, resulting in poor locality\nfor hierarchical GPU-CPU memory structures. GPUs, while\ncomputationally powerful,are constrained by memory capac-\nity. For instance, a 30B-parametermodel on a 24GB NVIDIA\nRTX 4090 GPU means only 37% of the model is on the GPU,\nshifting most computational tasks to the CPU. The CPU,\nwith higher memory but lower computational power, ends up\nhandling 98% of the total computational load (Figure 4b).\n3\nInsights into Locality in LLM Inference\nThis section introduces our insights into locality in the LLM\ninference procedure, highlighting two distinctive features.\n3.1\nInsight-1: Power-law Activation\n 0\n 20\n 40\n 60\n 80\n 100\n 0\n 20\n 40\n 60\n 80\n 100\nCDF(%)\nNeuron proportion (%)\nLLaMA(ReGLU)-70B\nOPT-30B\n26\n43\n(a)\n 0\n 20\n 40\n 60\n 80\n 100\nNeuron proportion (%)\nLLaMA(ReGLU)-70B\nOPT-30B\n26\n17\n(b)\nFigure 5: Cumulative distribution function (CDF) of neuron acti-\nvation in OPT-30B and LLaMA(ReGLU)-70B. (a) CDF in a single\nMLP layer. (b) CDF across the entire model. The X-axis shows neu-\nron proportion. The Y-axis represents the CDF of neuron activation.\nLLM inference exhibits a high degree of locality, indi-\ncating that a consistent group of neurons is frequently ac-\ntivated. Notwithstanding the input dependence of LLM ac-\ntivation sparsity, a power-law distribution is evident among\nactivated neurons. Figure 5a reveals that in the MLP layers\nof OPT-30B and LLaMA (ReGLU)-70B, 26% and 43% of\nneurons respectively are responsible for 80% of total activa-\ntions. These are termed hot-activated neurons. Conversely,\nthe activation of the remaining 74% and 57% of neurons is\ninput-dependent, classifying them as cold-activated neurons.\n2Since DejaVu only works for GPU, we modiﬁed it by using NVIDIA\nUniﬁed Memory (UM) [29] to fetch parameters from CPU memory.\nThis high locality is not conﬁned to a single layer but\nextends throughout the model. As illustrated in Figure 5b,\napproximately 17% of neurons in OPT-30B and 26% in\nLLaMA (ReGLU)-70B are responsible for 80% of the total\nactivations across all layers.\n3.2\nInsight-2: Fast In-CPU Computation\n 0\n 1\n 2\n1\n2\n4\n8\n16\n32\nTime(ms)\nBatch size\nLoad-And-Execute\nDirect-Execute\n(a) MLP layer\n 0\n 2\n 4\n1\n2\n4\n8\n16\n32\nBatch size\nLoad-And-Execute\nDirect-Execute\n(b) Attention layer\nFigure 6: Comparison of execution time for load-then-execute ver-\nsus direct-execute methods when 10% and 60% neuron weights of\none MLP and attention layer in OPT-30B are CPU-resident. The\nX-axis shows input batch sizes, and the Y-axis measures execution\ntime (ms). Load-then-execute involves transferring these neuron\nweights to GPU memory for computation, whereas direct-execute\ncomputes them directly on the CPU.\nIf activated neurons reside in CPU memory, computing\nthem on the CPU is faster than transferring them to the GPU,\nespecially with the small numberof activated neurons and the\nsmall batch sizes typical in local deployments. Modern CPUs\nwith vector extensions can efﬁciently handle such smaller\nmatrix computations.\nWe compared the time to load and compute 10%3 of the\nMLP layer and 60% of attention layer’s CPU-side neurons on\nthe GPU versus direct CPU execution in OPT-30B. Results\nin Figure 6 indicate that for batch sizes under 32, the time\ntaken to transfer the weights of these neurons and compute\nthem on the GPU (NVIDIA RTX 4090) exceeds the time\nrequired for calculation directly on the CPU using the AVX2\nvector extension.\n4\nPowerInfer Overview\nThis paper introduces PowerInfer, a low-latency LLM in-\nference system deployed in a PC equipped with a single\nconsumer-grade GPU. PowerInfer proposes a neuron-aware\nofﬂoading strategy and an inference engine by fully leverag-\ning the high locality insights described in §3. It utilizes both\nGPU and CPU for weight storage, accommodating LLMs of\nvarious sizes. This ofﬂoading approach, based on Insight-\n1, effectively exploits the power-law distribution of LLM\ninference. Speciﬁcally, PowerInfer preloads the GPU with\nweights for neurons that activate frequently, while less active\nneurons’ weights are kept on the CPU.\nTo reduce inference latency, the inference engine com-\nputes only neurons predicted as active by online predictors,\nskipping most inactive ones. Moreover, the preloading strat-\negy enables PowerInfer to allocate the bulk of inference tasks\n3While Insight-1 indicates that 43% of neurons account for 80% of the\ntotal activations in a single MLP layer, it is typically found that only about\n10% of its neurons are activated during an individual inference iteration.\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\n…\nProﬁler & Solver\nLLM\nGPU Mem\nGPU\n1\nCPU Mem\n2\n3\n3\n4\n4\nOﬄine\nOnline\nPCIe\nGPU\nExecutor\nCPU\nExecutor\nCPU\nCold\nHot\nCPU\nCore\nGPU\nSM\nPredicated\nActivation\nCalculation\nLoading\nFigure 7: The architecture overview and inference workﬂow of Pow-\nerInfer.\nto the GPU, given that hot-activated neurons that have been\nloaded on the GPU constitute a major fraction of activations.\nFor cold-activated neurons not in GPU memory, PowerIn-\nfer executes their computations on the CPU, eliminating the\nneed for weight transfers to the GPU (Insight-2).\n4.1\nArchitecture and Workﬂow\nFigure 7 presents an architectural overview of PowerInfer,\ncomprising both ofﬂine and online components. Due to the\nvariation in locality properties among different LLMs, the\nofﬂine component should proﬁle LLMs’ activation sparsity,\ndifferentiating between hot and cold neurons. In the online\nphase, the inference engine loads two types of neurons into\nboth GPU and CPU, serving LLM requests with low latency\nduring runtime.\nLLM Proﬁler and Policy Solver (Ofﬂine): This compo-\nnent includes an LLM proﬁler that collects activation data\nfrom inference processes using requests derived from gen-\neral datasets (e.g., C4 [32]). It monitors neuron activation\nacross all layers (Step ①), followed by a policy solver catego-\nrizing neurons as hot or cold. The solver aims to allocate fre-\nquently activated neurons to the GPU and others to the CPU.\nIt uses a neuron impact metric and hardware speciﬁcations\nto balance the workload, using integer linear programming\nto maximize the GPU’s impact metric for neurons (Step ②).\nNeuron-aware LLM Inference Engine (Online): Before\nprocessing user requests, the online engine assigns the two\ntypes of neurons to their respective processing units (Step ③),\nas per the ofﬂine solver’s output. During runtime, the engine\ncreates GPU and CPU executors, which are threads running\non the CPU side, to manage concurrent CPU-GPU compu-\ntations (Step ④). The engine also predicts neuron activation\nand skips non-activated ones. Activated neurons preloaded in\nGPU memory are processed there, while the CPU calculates\nand transfers results for its neurons to the GPU for integra-\ntion. The engine uses sparse-neuron-aware operators on both\nCPU and GPU, focusing on individual neuron rows/columns\nwithin matrices.\n1\n2\n4\n6\n3\n5\n7\nOnline Predictor\nCPU Mem\nLayer Output\nGPU Mem\nLayer Input\n1\n2\n6\n4\n5\n3\n7\nLayer i\nOﬄine\nSplit\nFigure 8: An illustrative example shows how PowerInfer calculates\ndifferent neurons for one LLM layer.\n4.2\nSingle Layer Example\nFigure 8 illustrates how PowerInfer coordinates GPU and\nCPU in processing a layer’s neurons. It classiﬁes neurons\nbased on ofﬂine data, assigning hot-activated ones (e.g., in-\ndices 3, 5, 7) to GPU memory and others to CPU memory.\nUpon receiving an input, a predictor identiﬁes which neurons\nin the current layer are likely to be activated. For instance,\nit predicts activation for neurons 3, 4, and 5. It is crucial to\nnote that hot-activated neurons, identiﬁed through ofﬂine sta-\ntistical analysis, may not consistently match the runtime ac-\ntivation behaviors. For example, neuron 7, though labeled as\nhot-activated, is forecasted to be inactive in this case.\nBoth CPU and GPU then process predicted active neurons,\nignoring inactive ones. The GPU computes neurons 3 and 5,\nwhile the CPU handles neuron 4. Once neuron 4’s compu-\ntation is complete, its output is sent to the GPU for result\nintegration.\n5\nNeuron-aware Inference Engine\nThis section presents a detailed introduction to the neuron-\naware inference engine in PowerInfer. We ﬁrst elaborate on\nthe design of activation predictors leveraged by PowerInfer\nin §5.1. Then, we elucidate the process of dividing and man-\naging neurons between the CPU and GPU in §5.2. Following\nthis, the design of the hybrid execution model within Power-\nInfer is described in §5.3. Lastly, we explain the details of\nneuron-aware operators used in PowerInfer in §5.4.\n5.1\nAdaptive Sparsity Predictors\nThe online inference engine in PowerInfer reduces computa-\ntional loads by only processing those neurons that are pre-\ndicted to be activated. This method was also used in De-\njaVu [21], which advocates for training a set of ﬁxed-size\nMLP predictors. Within each Transformer layer, DejaVu uti-\nlizes two separate predictors to forecast the activation of neu-\nrons in the self-attention and MLP blocks. Consequently, the\ninference computation is conﬁned to neurons anticipated to\nbe active.\nHowever, designing effective predictors for local deploy-\nments with limited resources is challenging, balancing pre-\ndiction accuracy and model size. These predictors,frequently\ninvoked for neuron activation prediction, should be stored in\nGPU memory for fast access. Yet, the considerable memory\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\n 0\n 50\n 100\n 150\n 200\n0.97\n0.975\n0.98\n0.985\n0.99\n0.995\nLow skewness\nHigh skewness\nParameters (MB)\nSparsity\nFigure 9: Correlation between predictor parameter size and layer\nsparsity at a guaranteed 95% accuracy level for OPT-175B. The\nX-axis represents sparsity, and the Y-axis represents the predictor\nparameter size. The bar indicates the average parameter size for the\nmodel in the corresponding sparsity, while the error bar reﬂects ﬂuc-\ntuations in the predictor parameter size due to skewness within the\nlayer.\nrequirements of numerous ﬁxed-size predictors can encroach\nupon the space needed for storing LLM parameters. For ex-\nample, predictors for the OPT-175B model require around\n27GB of GPU memory, surpassing an NVIDIA RTX 4090\nGPU’s capacity. On the other hand, naively reducing pre-\ndictor size may impair accuracy; a decrease from 480MB\nto 320MB in predictor size dropped its accuracy from 92%\nto 84%, further adversely affecting the overall LLM accu-\nracy (e.g., winogrande [35] task accuracy from 72.77% to\n67.96%).\nWe have observed that the size of predictors is inﬂuenced\nby two main factors: the sparsity of LLM layers and their\ninternal skewness. As shown in Figure 9, layers with higher\nactivation sparsity simplify the task of identifying activated\nneurons, allowing for smaller predictor models. In contrast,\nlayers with lower activation sparsity necessitate larger mod-\nels with more parameters, as accurately pinpointing activated\nneurons becomes increasingly challenging. Additionally, in\ncases of high skewness, where activations are heavily concen-\ntrated in a few neurons, even a compact predictor can achieve\nhigh accuracy.\nTo optimize for these factors, PowerInfer designs an iter-\native training method for non-ﬁxed-size predictors for each\nTransformer layer. The process begins by establishing a base-\nline model size based on the layer’s sparsity proﬁle (Fig-\nure 9). Subsequently, the model size is iteratively adjusted,\ntaking into account the internal activation skewness to main-\ntain accuracy. An MLP predictor typically comprises input,\nhidden, and output layers. Since the dimensions of the in-\nput and output layers are determined by the Transformer\nlayer’s structure, modiﬁcations primarily target the hidden\nlayer. During the iterative adjustments, the hidden layer’s di-\nmension is modiﬁed according to the observed skewness. For\nlayers exhibiting signiﬁcant skewness, the hidden layer size\nis reduced progressively until accuracy falls below 95%. Con-\nversely, for layers with minimal skewness, the dimension is\nincreased to improve accuracy. Through this approach, Pow-\nerInfer effectively limits predictor parameters to a mere 10%\nof the total LLM parameters.\n5.2\nNeuron Placement and Management\nWhen the ofﬂine solver determines a neuron placement pol-\nicy, the online inference engine of PowerInfer loads the\nmodel into the CPU and GPU memory as per the policy. For\neach layer, which may consist of multiple weight matrices,\nPowerInfer assigns each neuron to either the GPU or CPU\nbased on whether the neuron is hot-activated.\nEnsuring the accurate computation of these segmented\nneurons in their proper sequence is vital for precise results.\nTo this end, PowerInfer creates two neuron tables, one lo-\ncated in the CPU and the other in the GPU memory. These\ntables correlate each neuron to its original position in the\nmatrix. During the process of multiplying with an input\ntensor, each neuron interacts with its corresponding tensor\nvalue, guided by the mappings in the neuron tables. The ad-\nditional memory required for these neuron tables is relatively\ninsigniﬁcant, totaling only about 9MB for an LLM like OPT-\n175B, which needs 350GB of storage.\n5.3\nGPU-CPU Hybrid Execution\nGiven that PowerInfer processes only a limited number of\nneurons predicted to be active, such as less than 10% in an\nMLP layer, a potential method for GPU and CPU collab-\noration involves transferring cold-activated neuron weights\nfrom the CPU to the GPU for computation. However, as per\nInsight-2, the time spent transferring activated neurons to the\nGPU surpasses the time needed for direct computation on the\nCPU. Therefore, PowerInfer implements a GPU-CPU hybrid\nexecution model, wherein both units independently compute\ntheir respective activated neurons and then combine the re-\nsults on the GPU. This method effectively balances the com-\nputational workload, leveraging the strengths of each unit\nwhile reducing transfer time inefﬁciencies.\nBefore inference,PowerInfer constructs a computationally\ndirected acyclic graph (DAG) with each node representing\na computational LLM inference operator and stores it in a\nglobal queue in the CPU memory. Each operator in the queue\nis tagged with its prerequisite operators. During inference,\ntwo types of executors, pthreads created by the host OS, man-\nage calculations on both CPU and GPU. They pull operators\nfrom the global queue, check dependencies, and assign them\nto the appropriate processing unit. The GPU and CPU use\ntheir neuron-aware operators, with the GPU executor launch-\ning GPU operators using APIs like cudaLaunchKernel, and\nthe CPU executor coordinating unoccupied CPU cores for\ncalculations. Before executing an operator, the CPU executor\nalso determines the necessary thread count for parallel com-\nputation. To manage operator dependencies, especially when\na parent node of a CPU operator is processed on the GPU, a\nbarrier ensures GPU computations are complete before the\nCPU starts its operator.\nIn scenarios where activated neurons are split between\nGPU and CPU, synchronization between these processing\nunits also becomes crucial. After one unit ﬁnishes its neuron\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\ncalculations, it waits for the other to merge results. As GPU\nneurons are activated more frequently, PowerInfer assigns\nmerging operations to the GPU. To optimize synchroniza-\ntion overhead, a selective synchronization strategy is used,\nbypassing result synchronization when the CPU executor has\nno activated neurons, allowing it to proceed to subsequent\nblocks, thereby enhancing overall efﬁciency.\n5.4\nNeuron-aware Operator\nConsidering the activation sparsity in LLMs, matrix multi-\nplication operations can bypass inactive neurons and their\nweights, necessitating the use of sparse operators. However,\ncurrent sparse matrix multiplication tools, including state-of-\nthe-art sparse-aware compilers like SparTA [52] and Flash-\nLLM [45], as well as libraries like cuSPARSE [30] and\nSpunik [33], fall short in this regard. They either support\nonly static compilation of sparse-aware kernels or require dy-\nnamic conversion of sparse matrices into dense formats, lead-\ning to signiﬁcant performance overhead, especially with the\ndynamic sparsity in our scenario. Additionally, the dynamic\nJIT compiler PIT [51], though efﬁcient for general sparse\nmatrix multiplication on GPUs, is not suited for CPU-GPU\nhybrid execution where CPU computational capabilities are\nlimited.\nTo overcome these limitations, PowerInfer introduces\nneuron-aware operators that directly compute activated neu-\nrons and their weights on both GPU and CPU without the\nneed for runtime conversion to dense format. These operators\ndiffer from traditional ones as they focus on individual row/-\ncolumn vectors within a matrix rather than the entire matrix.\nThey ﬁrst determine a neuron’s activation status and then pro-\ncess it if predicted to be active, alongside the corresponding\nrow or column of the parameter matrix.\nNeuron-aware Operators for GPU: Despite vector-\nvector calculations being less efﬁcient than matrix-vectorcal-\nculations on GPU, neuron-aware operators based on vector-\nvector computation are advantageous when the batch size is\nsmall. They avoid unnecessary computations and memory\noperations associated with inactive neurons and do not need\ncostly matrix conversions. Furthermore, these operators al-\nlow all thread blocks to concurrently check neuron activa-\ntions and compute corresponding vectors if activated.\nNeuron-aware Operators for CPU: Neuron-aware oper-\nators are particularly beneﬁcial for CPUs, which generally\nhave lower parallelism and matrix computation efﬁciency.\nThe CPU executor assigns a neuron-aware operator to mul-\ntiple cores, dividing neurons into smaller batches for concur-\nrent activation checking. Each core processes only the ac-\ntivated neurons in its batch, optimizing vector-vector calcu-\nlations with hardware vector extensions like AVX2, widely\nsupported in modern CPUs.\n6\nNeuron Placement Policy\nTo fully unleash the computationalcapability of the GPU and\nCPU, PowerInfer’s ofﬂine component provides a placement\npolicy to guide the allocation of each neuron to either the\nGPU or CPU. This policy, output by a solver, controls neu-\nron placement within each layer, thereby deﬁning the run-\ntime computational workload for the respective processing\nunits. The solver considers a range of factors, including each\nneuron’s activation frequency, communication overhead, and\nthe computational capacities of the processing units, such as\ntheir memory sizes and bandwidths.\nThe solver deﬁnes an impact metric for each neuron to\nmodel its activation information. By integrating the neuron\nimpacts with the capabilities of different computing units,\nthe solver constructs an integer linear programming model\nto generate the optimal neuron placement.\n6.1\nOfﬂine Proﬁling\nBefore determining the placement of each neuron, the ofﬂine\nproﬁler of PowerInfer needs to gather runtime inference data\nfor each neuron. To achieve this, it deploys the LLM to han-\ndle requests generated from multiple general datasets, such\nas C4 [32] and Wikipedia [10]. To accurately measure acti-\nvation information, the proﬁler inserts a monitoring kernel\nafter each block within a Transformer layer. Additionally, it\nbuilds a neuron information table on the GPU, designed to\ntrack the activation count of each neuron.\nThis kernel checks whether each neuron in the layer gets\nactivated during the inference process and, if so, increments\nthe corresponding count in the neuron table. Once all re-\nquests have been processed, the proﬁler retrieves the activa-\ntion data from this table and passes it to the solver.\n6.2\nNeuron Impact Metric\nThe neuron impact metric measures each neuron’s contribu-\ntion to the LLM’s overall inference outcome,crucial for GPU\nneuron allocation. We calculate this metric effectively by\nleveraging the fact that proﬁled activation frequency mirrors\nruntime behavior accurately, provided the proﬁling involves\na substantial amount of input data. As Equation 1 shows, this\nmetric for a neuron is deﬁned by its activation frequency ob-\ntained during proﬁling.\nvi = fi\n∀i ∈N\n(1)\n6.3\nModeling of Neuron Placement\nBased on the neuron impact metric, PowerInfer utilizes a\nsolver to optimize the total impacts of all neurons in the GPU.\nThis cumulative impact is formulated as the objective func-\ntion, as deﬁned in Equation 2. This function is then input into\nan integer linear programming framework to identify a spe-\nciﬁc solution that maximizes the function. The binary vari-\nable ain, deﬁned in Equation 3 indicates whether the neuron\nn is placed on processing unit i.\nMaximize\nti = ∑\ne∈N\naie ∗ve∀i ∈{GPU}\n(2)\n∑\ni∈U\nain = 1\n∀n ∈N\n(3)\n7\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nSymbol\nType\nDescription\nL\nPar\nAll layers\nN\nPar\nAll neurons\nU\nPar\nCPU and GPU\nfi\nPar\nActivation frequency of neuron j\nNi\nPar\nNeuron in layer i\nvi\nPar\nNeuron impact for neuron j\nMi\nPar\nThe memory size for neuron i\nMCapj\nPar\nThe memory size for processing unit j\nBandwidthj\nPar\nThe memory bandwidth for processing unit j\nTsync\nPar\nThe time required for one synchronization between\nthe CPU and GPU\nK\nPar\nA large positive number\nain\nVar\nWhether neuron n is placed on processing unit U\nT\nj\nl\nVar\nThe time for computing one neuron in layer l on\nprocessing j\nCl\nVar\nThe minimum number of neurons required to be al-\nlocated on the GPU when the solver opts to split\nneurons in layer l\nyl\nVar\nBinary auxliary variable for layer l to facilitate the\nmodeling of conditional constraints\nTable 1: Terminology for ILP formulation. The Par represents the\nparameters gathered from the proﬁler or the expressions used to de-\nﬁne constraints, none of which need to be solved by the solver. The\nVar refers to the constraint and objective variables that emerge from\nthe modeling process, which need to be solved by the solver.\nWhen maximizing the objective function, the solver also\nneeds to consider two sets of constraints associated with the\ncommunication overhead between processing units and their\nhardware capabilities.\n6.3.1\nCommunication Constraint\nThe number of neurons preloaded onto the GPU is limited\nby the communication overhead within a layer, a constraint\ndictated by hardware PCIe bandwidth limitations. If too few\nneurons are preloaded, this overhead negates the computa-\ntional beneﬁts offered by the GPU. As a result, the solver\nmust identify a minimum number of neurons to allocate to\nthe GPU for processing. This ensures that neuron process-\ning on the GPU, including synchronization time, is more ef-\nﬁcient than CPU processing, as speciﬁed in Inequality 4. In\nthis inequality,Cl is the minimum count of neurons that must\nbe assigned to the GPU for layer l.\nWhen solving Inequality 4, it is essential to deﬁne both\nthe computation time for an individual neuron in layer l and\nthe intra-layer communication overhead, Tsync. In LLM infer-\nence, especially with smaller batch sizes, the process is pri-\nmarily limited by memory bandwidth. Therefore, the com-\nputation time for a neuron approximately equals the time\nneeded to access all of its weights once, as indicated in Equa-\ntion 5. With smaller batch sizes, the extent of intra-layer data\ntransfer tends to be consistent across layers, leading to a uni-\nform synchronization cost. Consequently, we describe Tsync\nas the proﬁled overhead for a single instance of intra-layer\ncommunication.\nCl ⋅T GPU\nl\n+Tsync ≤Cl ⋅TCPU\nl\n∀l ∈L\n(4)\nT j\ni = Mi/Bandwidth j\n∀j ∈D,∀i ∈L\n(5)\n6.3.2\nMemory Constraint\nNeuron placement is further constrained by the memory ca-\npacities of the processing units, as deﬁned in Inequality 6.\nMoreover, the solver ensures that when allocating neurons\nof a layer to the GPU, it either assigns at least the minimum\nnumber of neurons speciﬁed in Inequality 4 to offset com-\nmunication costs or opts not to allocate any neurons from\nthat layer to the GPU. Speciﬁcally, the number of neurons\nfor layer l on the GPU must either exceed Cl or be equal to\nzero.\nTo model this, we introduce an auxiliary binary variable,\nyl, which can be either 1 or 0. This variable determines\nwhether any neurons are assigned to the GPU for layer l.\nFor computational convenience, a sufﬁciently large number\nK is also introduced. Inequalities 7 and 8 are formulated\nto model this constraint. When yl is 1, indicating neuron\nplacement on the GPU for this layer, and given that K is\nadequately large, these two inequalities effectively become\nyl ≤∑e∈Nl aie ≤K. Conversely, if yl is set to 0, signifying\nno neuron placement on the GPU for layer l, the inequalities\nreduce to ∑e∈Nl aie = 0.\n∑\nn∈N\na jn ⋅Mn < MCap j\n∀j ∈U\n(6)\n∑\ne∈Nl\naie ≥Cl ⋅yl\n∀l ∈L,∀i ∈{GPU}\n(7)\n∑\ne∈Nl\naie ≤K ⋅yl\n∀l ∈L,∀i ∈{GPU}\n(8)\n6.3.3\nILP Optimization\nSubsequently, thesolver utilizes IntegerLinear Programming\n(ILP) to optimize the objective function, conforming to all\nthe constraints from Equation/Inequality 3 to 8. Given that\nILP problems are inherently NP-complete, directly solving\nthem for an LLM with hundreds of billions of parameters\nposes a considerable computational challenge. To expedite\nthe process and achieve an approximate solution, the primary\nstrategy involves aggregating neurons within each layer into\nbatches for collective placement analysis. Speciﬁcally, the\nsolver groups 64 neurons with similar impacts from a layer\ninto a single batch. This batching strategy dramatically re-\nduces the total neuron count, N, from several millions to\nroughly tens of thousands, thereby signiﬁcantly decreasing\nthe time to solve the ILP problem to approximately 10 sec-\nonds.\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\n7\nImplementation\nThe online inference engine of PowerInfer has been imple-\nmented by incorporating an additional 4,200 lines of C++\nand CUDA code into llama.cpp [14], a state-of-the-art open-\nsource LLM inference framework designed for PCs. The ex-\ntensions made by PowerInfer include modiﬁcations to the\nmodel loader for distributing an LLM across GPU and CPU,\nfollowing the guidance from the ofﬂine solver’s outputs. We\nhave also optimized the inference engine for GPU-CPU hy-\nbrid execution and introduced 10 neuron-aware operators for\nboth processing units. All other components and functional-\nities of llama.cpp remains unchanged. For instance, the KV\ncache continues to reside in CPU memory, allowing more\nGPU memory for hot-activated neurons, as its access has\nminimal impact on inference latency, particularly in small\nbatch sizes. Furthermore, around 400 lines of Python code\nwere added to the transformers framework [44], enabling it\nto function as an ofﬂine proﬁler and solver for PowerInfer.\nThe current implementation of PowerInfer supports a\nrange of mainstream LLM families with varying parameter\nsizes, including the OPT [49] family (from 7B to 175B pa-\nrameters), the LLaMA [42] family (7B to 70B), and Falcon-\n40B [2]. For these models, PowerInfer utilizes DejaVu [21]\nto train online activation predictors, which has been en-\nhanced with an adaptive training method. While training an\nLLM is a lengthy process, often taking several hours, it is\na one-time task. The duration of this process can be signiﬁ-\ncantly reduced by utilizing multiple high-end GPUs.\n8\nEvaluation\n8.1\nExperimental Setup\nHardware. To demonstrate the generalization of PowerIn-\nfer across various hardware setups, experiments were con-\nducted on two distinct PC conﬁgurations, representing both\nhigh-end and low-end hardware scenarios:\n• PC-High: Equipped with an Intel i9-13900K processor\n(eight physical cores at 5.4GHz) and 192GB host mem-\nory (memory bandwidth of 67.2 GB/s). This conﬁgura-\ntion includes an NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU (24G) with a\nmemory bandwidth of 1TB/s and operates with a PCIe\n4.0 interface (64GB/s bandwidth).\n• PC-Low: Features an Intel i7-12700K processor (eight\nphysical cores at 4.9GHz), coupled with 64GB of host\nmemory (memory bandwidth 38.4 GB/s). It also in-\ncludes an NVIDIA RTX 2080Ti GPU (11G) with a\nmemory bandwidth of 616GB/s and utilizes PCIe 3.0\ninterface (32GB/s bandwidth).\nModels. We use a range of OPT [49] models with parameters\nfrom 6.7B to 175B, as well as Falcon(ReLU)-40B [38] and\nLLaMA(ReGLU)-70B [39] models. Notably, the 175B pa-\nrameter model is comparable in size to the GPT-3 model [5].\nFor our experiments, all models in our experiments use FP16\nand INT4 quantized parameters, with intermediate activa-\ntions in FP32, consistent with recent LLM research prac-\ntices [12,47].\nWorkloads. The workloads for our experiments are derived\nfrom ChatGPT prompts [28] and Alpaca [41] datasets, cover-\ning a wide spectrum of language model uses. These datasets\nconsist of input and output texts typical of real LLM ser-\nvices. ChatGPT prompts include user interactions with Chat-\nGPT [31], and Alpaca features instruction sets generated by\nGPT3.5 through self-instruction.\nBaseline\nSystem.\nWe\ncompare\nPowerInfer\nwith\nllama.cpp [14], a state-of-the-art local LLM inference\nframework. To facilitate this comparison, we extended\nllama.cpp to support the OPT model, as it lacks native\ncompatibility. While other alternatives like FlexGen [37]\nand DejaVu [21] exist, they exhibit higher latency in the\nlatency-sensitive scenarios discussed in\nthis paper, as\nanalyzed in §2.2. Therefore, llama.cpp serves as the more\nrelevant benchmark for our evaluation.\nKey Metrics. As we focus on low latency setting, our pri-\nmary evaluation metric is end-to-end generation speed, quan-\ntiﬁed as the average number of tokens generated per second\n(tokens/s). It is calculated by dividing the total count of gen-\nerated tokens by the end-to-end response time, offering a pre-\ncise measure of the response generation process’s efﬁciency.\n8.2\nEnd-to-End Performance\n0\n4\n8\n12\n16\nSpeedup\n1.0\n2.7\n1.6\n13\n1.6\n16\n0.3\n1.2\n0.6\n2.2\n0.6\n2.3\n0.8\n4.4\n1.1\n11\n1.1\n13\n0.4\n0.6\n0.6\n1.7\n0.6\n2.0\nOPT 30B\nOPT 66B\nFalcon 40B\nLLaMA 70B\nInput length=64\nllama.cpp\nPowerInfer\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\nOutput length\n0\n4\n8\n12\nSpeedup\n0.9\n2.6\n1.6\n13\n1.6\n16\n0.2\n0.8\n0.5\n2.1\n0.6\n2.3\n0.8\n1.7\n1.1\n8.8\n1.1\n11\n0.4\n0.7\n0.6\n1.8\n0.6\n1.9\nInput length=128\nFigure 10: Speedup of various models on PC-High in FP16 format.\nThe X axis indicates the output length. The performance superiority of PowerInfer becomes\nmore pronounced as the number of output tokens increases\nsince the generation phase plays a more signiﬁcant role in the\noverall inference time. In this phase, a small number of neu-\nrons are activated on both CPU and GPU, leading to fewer\nunnecessary computations compared to llama.cpp. For exam-\nple, in the case of OPT-30B, only around 20% of neurons\nare activated for each token generated, with the majority pro-\ncessed on the GPU, a beneﬁt of PowerInfer’s neuron-aware\ninference engine.\nFigure 11 shows that on a lower-end PC (PC-Low), Pow-\nerInfer still attains considerable performance enhancement\nover llama.cpp, averaging a speedup of 5.01× and peaking at\n7.06×. However, these improvements are smaller compared\nto those on a higher-end PC (PC-High), primarily due to the\n11GB GPU memory limitation of PC-Low. This limitation af-\nfects the number of neurons that can be allocated to the GPU,\nparticularly for models with around 30B parameters or more,\nleading to a greater dependence on the CPU for processing a\nlarger number of activated neurons.\nFigure 12 presents the distribution of neuron loads be-\ntween the CPU and GPU for both PowerInfer and llama.cpp.\nNeuron loads refer to the proportion of activated neuron com-\nputations carried out by each processing unit. Notably, on\nPC-High, PowerInfer signiﬁcantly increases the GPU’s share\nof neuron load, from an average of 20% to 70%. This indi-\ncates that the GPU processes 70% of activated neurons. How-\never, in cases where the model’s memory requirements far\nexceed the GPU’s capacity, such as running a 60GB model\non an 11GB 2080Ti GPU, the GPU’s neuron load is reduced\nto 42%. This decrease is due to the GPU’s limited memory,\nwhich is insufﬁcient to host all hot-activated neurons, neces-\nsitating that the CPU compute a portion of these neurons.\nIn scenarios involving long input prompts with relatively\nshort output lengths, which are less common [28], PowerIn-\nfer demonstrates only limited performance gains. In such sit-\n 0\n 20\n 40\n 60\n 80\n 100\nOPT-30B\nOPT-66B\nOPT-175B(INT4)\nNeuron Load Breakdown (%)\nllama.cpp-CPU\nllama.cpp-GPU\n(a) PC-High\nOPT-13B\nOPT-66B(INT4)\nOPT-30B\nPowerInfer-CPU\nPowerInfer-GPU\n(b) PC-Low\nFigure 12: Neuron load distribution on CPU and GPU during in-\nference. The yellow block refers to llama.cpp, and blue block refers\nto PowerInfer.\nuations, the prompt phase, where a substantial number of to-\nkens are processed simultaneously, becomes a crucial factor\nin determining inference speed. This results in each token\nactivating a unique set of neurons, substantially diminishing\nactivation sparsity. As a consequence, the CPU becomes the\nprimary bottleneck in the inference process, tasked with pro-\ncessing a considerable number of cold-activated neurons but\nconstrained by its computational capabilities.\nInference with Quantization. Figure 13 illustrates that Pow-\nerInfer effectively supports LLMs that are compressed using\nINT4 quantization. On a high-end PC (PC-High), PowerIn-\nfer delivers responses at an average speed of 13.20 token-\ns/s, reaching a peak of 29.08 tokens/s. The average speedup\nachieved compared with llama.cpp is 2.89×, with a maxi-\nmum of 4.28×. On a lower-end setup (PC-Low), the aver-\nage speedup is 5.01×, peaking at 8.00×. The reduction in\nmemory requirements due to quantization enables PowerIn-\nfer to more efﬁciently manage larger models. For instance,\nin our experiment with the OPT-175B model on PC-High,\nPowerInfer nearly reaches two tokens per second, surpassing\nllama.cpp by a factor of 2.66×.\nBatching Inference. We also evaluate the end-to-end infer-\nence performance of PowerInfer with different batch sizes, as\nshown in Figure 14. PowerInfer demonstrates a signiﬁcant\nadvantage when the batch size is smaller than 32, achiev-\ning an average 6.08× improvement in performance com-\npared with llama.cpp. As the batch size increases, the speed-\nup ratio offered by PowerInfer decreases. This reduction is\nattributed to the diminished sparsity of model joint activa-\ntions. However, even with the batch size set to 32, PowerIn-\nfer still maintains a considerable speedup, achieving a 4.38×\nspeedup.\n8.3\nAblation Studies\n8.3.1\nPerformance Breakdown\nFigure 15 breaks down the contributions of each PowerIn-\nfer component to the overall performance speedup. Using\na step-by-step integration method, we progressively incorpo-\nrate PowerInfer features into llama.cpp. First, we add Power-\nInfer’s predictors and neuron-aware operators into llama.cpp\n(labeled \"+PO\"), enabling computation of only activated neu-\nrons on both GPU and CPU. Yet, +PO still adheres to layer-\nwise computation, where each layer is processed entirely by\neither GPU or CPU.\nBuilding on +PO, we introduce PowerInfer’s hybrid in-\n10\n\n\n=== PDF page 11 ===\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\nOutput length\n0\n2\n4\n6\nSpeedup\n1.9\n7.6\n2.9\n12\n2.9\n12\n0.5\n1.2\n0.7\n1.9\n0.7\n1.8\n15\n22\n17\n29\n17\n27\n2.4\n6.4\n3.2\n9.5\n3.2\n10\nOPT 66B\nOPT 175B\nFalcon 40B\nLLaMA 70B\nPC-High\nllama.cpp\nPowerInfer\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\n8\n128\n512\nOutput length\n0\n2\n4\n6\n8\n10\nSpeedup\n2.9\n7.6\n3.9\n13\n3.8\n12\n0.8\n3.3\n1.1\n5.3\n1.1\n5.0\n1.7\n11\n2.0\n16\n2.0\n15\n1.0\n2.9\n1.1\n4.2\n1.1\n4.2\nOPT 30B\nOPT 66B\nFalcon 40B\nLLaMA 70B\nPC-Low\nFigure 13: Speedup of different models on PC-High and PC-Low in\nINT4 format. The\nupper row of the ﬁgure presents performance on PC-High, while the\nlower row details those on PC-Low.\n 0\n 8\n 16\n 24\n 32\n 40\n1\n4\n8\n16\n32\nTokens/s\nBatch size\nllama.cpp\nPowerInfer\n10.73X\n5.73X\n4.85X\n4.69X\n4.38X\nFigure 14: Batch inference speedup of Falcon-40B on PC-High.\nThe X axis indicates the request batch size, the Y axis represents the\nend-to-end token generation speed (tokens/s). The number above\neach bar shows the speedup compared with llama.cpp.\nference engine (denoted \"+Engine\"), which allows neuron-\naware operators to process neurons within the same layer si-\nmultaneously on both GPU and CPU. +Engine uses a naive\nneuron partitioning policy that assigns frequently activated\nneurons to the GPU. The ﬁnal step involves integrating\nour optimized policy (\"+Policy\"), formulated by the ofﬂine\nsolver as described in §6, into the +Engine setup, showcas-\ning the full capabilities of PowerInfer.\nThe initial integration of +PO into llama.cpp yields per-\nformance boosts of 1.98× and 2.00× for OPT-30B and\nOPT-66B, respectively, primarily by reducing unnecessary\ninactive neurons. +Engine further escalates these gains to\n9.97× and 3.43×, thanks to precise neuron placement and\nintra-layer calculations that signiﬁcantly increase the GPU’s\ncomputational share. Finally, incorporating +Policy results\nin improvements of 10.47× and 3.67×. The enhancement\nachieved by our policy lies in its ability to ﬁnely balance the\nintra-layer communication overhead. The naive partitioning\npolicy in +Engine overlooks the GPU-CPU intra-layer com-\nmunication, often offsetting the beneﬁts of assigning high-\nfrequency activation neurons to the GPU. Conversely, our\npolicy in PowerInfer more adeptly balances processing loads\nand communication costs between the CPU and GPU.\n 0\n 2\n 4\n 6\n 8\n 10\n 12\nOPT-30B\nOPT-66B\nSpeedup\nllama.cpp\n+PO\n+Engine\n+Policy\nFigure 15: Performance breakdown for each component of Power-\nInfer on PC-High.\n 0\n 0.5\n 1\n 1.5\n 2\n 2.5\n 3\n 0\n 0.2\n 0.4\n 0.6\n 0.8\n 1\nExecution Time (ms)\nSparse operator on CPU\ndense\nPyTorch sparse\nPowerInfer\n 0\n 0.1\n 0.2\n 0.3\n 0\n 0.2\n 0.4\n 0.6\n 0.8\n 1\nSparse operator on GPU\ndense\nPIT\nPowerInfer\nFigure 16: Comparing Neuron-aware operator with different\nsparse operators on PC-Low. The X axis indicates the sparsity level,\nthe Y axis represents the execution time(ms).\n8.3.2\nNeuron-aware Operator Performance\nThis section evaluates the performance of PowerInfer’s\nsparse operators on both CPU and GPU across various spar-\nsity levels. We benchmark PowerInfer against leading sparse\nlibraries: for CPU benchmarks, we use PyTorch sparse, the\nstate-of-the-art sparse kernels within PyTorch, as our base-\nline. In GPU, PowerInfer is compared with PIT [51]. Given\nthat the sparsity in LLMs is typically based on neuron gran-\nularity, our experiments are speciﬁcally designed to evaluate\nsparse matrices of this nature. We focus on sparse matrix-\nvector multiplication using a [4096, 4096] × [4096, 1] con-\nﬁguration, a common setup in local LLM inference [6]. To\nadjust sparsity, we introduce zero values to matrix rows.\nFigure 16 shows that PowerInfer’s operator achieves\nnearly linear acceleration with increasing sparsity levels, a\nstark contrast to dense matrix computations. On the CPU,\ntraditional sparse operators do not outperform dense com-\nputation until sparsity surpasses 87%. However, PowerIn-\nfer’s CPU operator outperforms dense matrix multiplication\neven at sparsity levels below 10%. For the GPU, PowerIn-\nfer matches PIT in performance. Its primary advantage, how-\never, is its uniﬁed CPU-GPU framework. This design allows\nfor ﬂexible execution of sparse operators on both process-\ning units, unlike PIT, which is optimized solely for GPU-\nbased sparse matrix multiplication and does not support hy-\nbrid CPU-GPU environments.\n8.3.3\nPredictor Overhead\nThe execution time of the online predictors for different mod-\nels is also measured, as depicted in Figure 17. On average,\nthe execution of predictors constitutes less than 10% of the\ntotal inference time in PowerInfer. This efﬁciency is primar-\nily due to the adaptive methods used in constructing sparsity\npredictors, which minimizes computational load. Moreover,\nthese dense-model predictors are incorporated into Power-\nInfer’s solver for neuron placement decisions, with a prefer-\nence for allocating them to the GPU. This strategy effectively\n11\n\n\n=== PDF page 12 ===\n 0\n 20\n 40\n 60\n 80\n 100\nOPT-7B\nOPT-13B\nOPT-30B(INT4)\nOPT-30B\nBreakdown (%)\nInference\nPrediction\nFigure 17: End-to-end prediction overhead of PowerInfer on PC-\nLow. The X axis represents various models, while the Y-axis dis-\nplays the percentage breakdown between predictor overhead and\nLLM inference time.\n 0\n 10\n 20\n 30\nOPT-30B\nFalcon-40B\nAverage tokens/s\n(a) Input length = 1\nllama.cpp(4090)\nPowerInfer(4090)\nvLLM(A100)\n18%\n93%\n23%\n92%\n \n \n \n \nOPT-30B\nFalcon-40B\n(b) Input length = 64\n28%\n93%\n29%\n94%\nFigure 18: Generation speed of NVIDIA RTX 4090 compared with\nsingle A100. The X axis represents various models, while the Y-axis\nrepresents end-to-end generation speed (tokens/s) under various in-\nference framework. The percentages within the arrows represent the\nslowdown relative to vLLM on the A100.\nleverages the parallel processing capabilities of GPUs, fur-\nther minimizing the overhead associated with the predictors.\n8.3.4\nPerformance Comparison with A100\nIn our study, we analyze the extent to which PowerInfer re-\nduces the performance gap between a consumer-grade GPU\nand its top-tier server-grade counterpart. Therefore, we eval-\nuate the generation speed of PowerInfer, deployed on PC-\nHigh, in comparison to the performance of llama.cpp and\nvLLM [18] executed on a single 80GB NVIDIA A100 GPU.\nWe chose the OPT-30B and Falcon-40B models for compari-\nson, considering their exact memory requirements matching\nprecisely with the capacity of the A100 GPU. Our evaluation\nused input lengths of 1 and 64 to measure pure generation\nspeed and conversational interactions, respectively.\nFigure 18a demonstrates that PowerInfer signiﬁcantly nar-\nrows the performance gap between the NVIDIA 4090 and\nA100 in generation tasks with input length 1. On PC-High,\nllama.cpp lags behind vLLM on the A100 by 93% and 92%\nfor OPT-30B and Falcon-40B, respectively, but PowerInfer\nreduces this to 18% and 23%. Figure 18b shows that despite\nreduced cumulative sparsity in the prompt phase, PowerInfer\nstill reduces the performance gap to 28% and 29%. The re-\nmaining disparity mainly stems from the CPU’s considerable\ncomputational load, which has become a bottleneck.\n8.4\nLLM Accuracy\nSince PowerInfer selectively omits neurons predicted to be\ninactive, we investigated whether this approach affects the\ninference accuracy of LLMs. Table 2 compares the accu-\nracy of models from the OPT, Falcon (ReLU), and LLaMA\n(ReGLU) families, both with and without differentiating ac-\ntivated/inactivated neurons, across a variety of downstream\ntasks. The results show that PowerInfer causes negligible\nTable 2: Comparison of LLM accuracy between PowerInfer-\noptimized models (termed as \"model-sparse\") and their original\ncounterparts. COPA [34] serves as a QA dataset focused on eval-\nuating causal reasoning skills in language models. PIQA [4] and\nWinogrande [35] are designed for probing common sense reason-\ning abilities and the understanding of physical world interactions in\nLLMs. RTE [15] is used to assess natural language understanding\nvia textual entailment.\nPIQA\nWinogrande\nRTE\nCOPA\nOPT-7B\n75.78%\n65.19%\n55.23%\n81.00%\nOPT-7B-sparse\n75.67%\n65.51%\n55.96%\n81.00%\nOPT-13B\n76.01%\n64.96%\n58.12%\n85.00%\nOPT-13B-sparse\n76.28%\n65.98%\n56.32%\n84.00%\nOPT-30B\n77.58%\n68.43%\n58.40%\n82.00%\nOPT-30B-sparse\n77.48%\n67.56%\n59.93%\n86.00%\nOPT-66B\n78.62%\n68.82%\n60.29%\n86.00%\nOPT-66B-sparse\n79.16%\n67.80%\n61.01%\n87.00%\nOPT-175B\n79.65%\n72.77%\n59.93%\n88.00%\nOPT-175B-sparse\n79.26%\n72.38%\n61.98%\n87.00%\nFalcon(ReLU)-40B\n81.23%\n75.45%\n66.43%\n92.00%\nFalcon(ReLU)-40B-sparse\n81.01%\n75.92%\n64.62%\n93.00%\nLLaMA(ReGLU)-70B\n82.01%\n75.93%\n75.81%\n89.00%\nLLAMA(ReGLU)-70B-sparse\n82.05%\n75.53%\n77.62%\n90.00%\nloss in inference accuracy, regardless of the model size or\ntype of task, consistent with previous research ﬁndings [21].\nAlthough the predictors in each Transformer layer maintain\nan accuracy rate above 95%, they may occasionally miss\nsome active neurons. As a result, there are minor ﬂuctuations\nin LLM accuracy, leading to slight decreases or sometimes\neven increases in performanceon speciﬁc downstream tasks.\n9\nRelated Work\nLLM Activation Sparsity: Recent advancements like De-\njaVu [21], PIT [51], and brainstorm [8] are crucial to opti-\nmizing LLM inference, akin to PowerInfer. DejaVu [21] pro-\nposes enhancing inference speed through activation sparsity\nprediction, while PowerInfer leverages a power-law distribu-\ntion in neuron activations, focusing on GPU computation of\nfrequently activated neurons. PIT [51] accelerates GPU tasks\nby converting sparse to dense matrices. However, these meth-\nods, primarily exploiting GPU sparsity, face limitations in\nresource-constrained local environments.\nLLM Weight Sparsity: Model pruning [16, 17, 24], re-\nducing parameter count by setting some weights to zero,\nis exempliﬁed by SparseGPT [11] and Wanda [40], achiev-\ning nearly 50% unstructured sparsity. SparTA [52] leverages\nboth sparse tensor and SIMT cores by dividing sparse ma-\ntrices. Flash-LLM [45] introduces a \"Load-as-Sparse and\nCompute-as-Dense\" approach for tensor core SpMM. How-\never, these methods, orthogonal to LLMs’ intrinsic sparse ac-\ntivations, usually incur accuracy losses and wall-clock model\nacceleration challenges [27]. This is in contrast to the natu-\nral sparse activations utilized by PowerInfer, which maintain\nperformance and enhance computational efﬁciency.\nSpeculative LLM Inference: speculative inference [6,\n12\n\n\n=== PDF page 13 ===\n13, 43] can also be leveraged to serve models exceeding\nGPU memory. Speculative decoding [7] uses a smaller, faster\nmodel to pre-decode tokens, later validated by the main\nmodel in a batch, reducing steps and CPU-GPU communica-\ntion. SpecInfer [26], as another example, effectively reduces\nthe number of LLM decoding steps and the overall communi-\ncation between CPU DRAM and GPU HBM. While separate\nfrom our focus, integrating speculative inference into Power-\nInfer could further boost LLM inference speed.\nLLM-Speciﬁc Serving Optimizations: The prominence\nof Transformers has led to specialized serving systems [9,\n36, 53]. Orca [47] introduces iteration-level scheduling.\nvLLM [18] implements Paged Attention for token storage\nin varied GPU memory addresses, overcoming KV cache’s\ncontinuous storage limit. While vLLM effectively mitigates\nthe issue of severe GPU memory fragmentation, it does not\naddress the challenge of deploying models on PCs where the\nentire model cannot ﬁt within the available GPU memory.\n10\nConclusion\nPowerInfer is a fast inference system optimized for LLMs\nthat exploits the locality property in LLM inference. It uti-\nlizes adaptive predictors and neuron-aware operators for\nneuron activation and computational sparsity. PowerInfer\nachieves up to 11.69× faster LLM inference compared to sys-\ntems like llama.cpp, without compromising accuracy.\nReferences\n[1] Abien Fred Agarap.
